Immediate Recall of List 3

For correct List 3 recall, a 2 × 2 ANOVA with the between-subjects factors interim activity (restudy vs. testing) and attention (full vs. divided) revealed a significant main effect of interim activity, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 33.996, 𝑝𝑝 < .001, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .250 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵 10 > 100, relative to the null model), and a significant main effect of attention, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 72.873, 𝑝𝑝 < .001, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .428 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵 10 > 100, relative to the null model). Importantly, the interaction between the two factors was also significant, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 3.978, 𝑝𝑝 = .049, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .030 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 1.454, relative to the two-maineffects model). Post-hoc comparisons indicated a reliable forward testing effect under full attention, 𝑡𝑡(46) = 4.596, 𝑝𝑝 < .001, 𝑑𝑑 = 1.327 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵 10 > 100), and a reduced but still significant forward testing effect under divided attention, 𝑡𝑡(46) = 3.475, 𝑝𝑝 = .001, 𝑑𝑑 = 1.003 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 28.268; see Fig. 1).

---Figure 1 about here ---Regarding prior-list intrusions in the List 3 recall test, a 2 × 2 ANOVA revealed a significant main effect of interim activity, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 5.876, 𝑝𝑝 = .017, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .048 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 2.891, relative to the null model), but neither a significant main effect attention, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 0.092, 𝑝𝑝 = .763, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝 2 < .001 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 4.481, relative to the null model), nor a significant interaction between the two factors, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 0.367, 𝑝𝑝 = .546, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝 2 < .001 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 3.208, relative to the two-main-effects model). These results indicate a reliable forward testing effect on prior-list intrusions that was unaffected by the attention manipulation.

Final Recall of Lists 2 and 1

Two 2 × 2 ANOVAs with the between-subjects factors interim activity (restudy vs. testing) and attention (full vs. divided) were conducted for List 2 and List 1 recall. For List 2, the ANOVA revealed no significant main effect of interim activity, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 0.823, 𝑝𝑝 = .367, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝 2 < .001 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 3.224, relative to the null model), no main effect of attention, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 0.490, 𝑝𝑝 = .486, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝 2 < .001 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 3.744, relative to the null model), and no interaction between the two factors, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 0.490, 𝑝𝑝 = .486, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝 2 < .001 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 2.647, relative to the two-main-effects model). Similarly, for List 1, no significant main effects of interim activity, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 0.090, 𝑝𝑝 = .765, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝 2 < .001 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 4.476, relative to the null model), or attention, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 0.023, 𝑝𝑝 = .881, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝 2 < .001 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 4.613, relative to the null model), and no significant interaction were observed, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 1.564, 𝑝𝑝 = .214, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .006 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 1.861, relative to the two-main-effects model).

Discussion

Experiment 1 examined whether dividing attention during encoding of the target material modulates the benefits of interim testing for new learning when using unrelated word lists for study. Replicating prior work, interim testing enhanced recall of subsequently studied material and reduced prior-list intrusions. Dividing attention during study of the target list substantially impaired recall overall and attenuated the benefit of interim testing on recall of the target list, although the effect remained reliable under divided attention. These findings suggest that attentional engagement during encoding of the target material contributes to the benefits of interim testing for new learning. They are consistent with the view that dividing attention during target-list encoding reduces the extent to which interim testing can facilitate subsequent encoding. Interim testing also reduced prior-list intrusions, but this effect was not modulated by the attention manipulation, suggesting that interference reduction may contribute to the benefits of interim testing in a manner that is less dependent on attentional resources during target encoding. Finally, no backward testing effect emerged for the previously studied lists. This finding is consistent with evidence that the mnemonic benefits of retrieval practice over restudy for previously studied information typically emerge only after longer retention intervals (Roediger & Karpicke, 2006; Toppino & Cohen, 2009).

Experiment 2

Experiment 2 extended the investigation to more educationally relevant learning materials by using related prose passages instead of unrelated word lists. The texts were adopted from recently developed materials by Kriechbaum and Bäuml (in press) and consisted of three short expository passages on fundamental mechanisms of human memory and forgetting. These materials were selected because they have been validated in previous forward-testing research with prose materials (Kriechbaum & Bäuml, in press), allowing direct comparison with prior findings. In contrast to the unrelated word lists used in Experiment 1, the passages contained conceptually related information and thus more closely resembled the kinds of materials encountered in educational settings. In addition to the change in materials, two further modifications were implemented to increase ecological validity. First, the participant sample included both university students and non-students to increase the generalizability of the findings across the adult population and therefore covered a broader adult age range than in Experiment 1; importantly, prior work indicates that the benefits of interim testing for subsequent learning are largely independent of age within adulthood (Pastötter & Bäuml, 2019). Second, the attention manipulation employed a more complex and naturalistic secondary task than in Experiment 1.

Participants listened to a fictional news report about the Summer Olympic Games that contained numerous single-digit numbers referring, for instance, to scores or statistics. While studying the text material, participants were required to monitor the auditory stream and raise one arm whenever they detected an odd digit, while withholding a response for even digits, thereby inducing sustained divided attention during encoding.

Method

We report how we determined our sample size, all data exclusions (if any), all manipulations, and all measures in Experiment 2. Data were collected between May 2025 and November 2025.

Participants

Participants in Experiment 2 were 96 adults, including 74 students from Trier University and 22 non-students, resulting in a broader adult age range than in Experiment 1 (mean age = 27.58 years, SD = 11.70, range = 19 -74 years; 57 females, 39 males). The sample size was identical to that of Experiment 1. All participants provided written informed consent prior to participation, and the students received course credit. The study was conducted in accordance with the ethical standards of the ethics committee of Trier University. Information on race/ethnicity was not collected and therefore cannot be reported.

Material

The learning material consisted of three conceptually related German prose passages identical to those used in Experiment 2 of Kriechbaum and Bäuml (in press).

Experimental Design

Experiment 2 employed a 2 × 2 between-subjects design with the factors interim activity (restudy vs. testing) and attention (full vs. divided). The design was conceptually identical to that of Experiment 1 but used prose passages instead of unrelated word lists as learning material. In the testing condition, participants completed two free-recall tests for each of the first two passages, whereas in the restudy condition, participants reread each passage for an equivalent amount of time. By including two recall/restudy cycles, we closely followed the procedure established by Kriechbaum and Bäuml (in press), ensuring comparability with that work. In the full-attention condition, Passage 3 was studied without any concurrent task. In the dividedattention condition, participants studied Passage 3 while simultaneously monitoring an auditory stream containing single-digit numbers and were instructed to raise one arm whenever an odd digit occurred.

Procedure

Participants were tested individually in a quiet laboratory environment. After providing informed consent, participants received standardized instructions explaining that they would study three passages and that their memory for the material would be tested later. The experiment began with the study of Passage 1, which participants read for 6 min. After the study phase, participants completed a 30-s distractor task in which they counted backward in steps of three from a threedigit number. Next followed two 5-min phases separated by a 2-min arithmetic distractor task. In the testing condition, participants were instructed to write down everything they could remember from the passage during each of the two phases. In the restudy condition, participants reread the passage during the two phases. The identical procedure was then applied to Passage 2.

The attention manipulation was introduced during study of Passage 3, which participants studied for 6 min. In the full-attention condition, participants studied the passage without any concurrent task. In the divided-attention condition, participants simultaneously listened to the Olympic news recording and were instructed to monitor the auditory stream for single-digit numbers. Whenever an odd digit occurred, they raised either their left or right arm, while no response was required for even digits. This task created sustained divided attention during encoding of the critical passage. After study of Passage 3, participants again completed the 30-s backward-counting distractor task, followed by a 5-min free-recall test for Passage 3. Subsequently, participants performed a brief visual distractor task in which they searched for differences between pairs of pictures for 3 min. Finally, participants completed the final knowledge transfer test on the contents of the passages, for which they were given up to 15 min. The entire experiment lasted approximately 70 min.

Data Analysis

Data were analyzed using the same statistical procedures as in Experiment 1. Percentage of correctly recalled idea units from Passage 3 (maximum = 14 idea units) and performance on the final knowledge transfer test (maximum = 10 points) were examined as dependent variables in frequentist and Bayesian ANOVAs and post-hoc t tests. All responses were scored by one rater.

In addition, as a reliability check, the responses of 40 participants were independently scored by a second rater. Inter-rater reliability was very high for both recall performance for Passage 3, 𝑟𝑟 = .950, 𝑝𝑝 < .001, and performance on the final knowledge transfer test, 𝑟𝑟 = .950, 𝑝𝑝 < .001. The data and analysis script are available as a JASP file on OSF (https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/WDFHK).

Results

Secondary Task

Participants made on average 27.19 correct responses to odd digits (hits) out of 30 (𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆 = 2.56).

They produced 0.85 false alarms on average, that is, arm raises in response to even digits (out of 30; 𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆 = 1.34). Accuracy, calculated as hits minus false alarms, was on average 26.33 (𝑆𝑆𝑆𝑆 = 3.06) and was clearly above chance level, as indicated by a one-sample t test against the test value of 0, 𝑡𝑡(47) = 59.583, 𝑝𝑝 < .001, 𝑑𝑑 = 8.600 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵 10 > 100).

Immediate Recall of Passages 1 and 2

The results of all recall tests are presented in Table 2. Immediate recall of Passages 1 and 2 was assessed only in the testing condition. Note that each passage was tested twice after study. A 2 × 2 × 2 repeated-measures ANOVA with the within-subjects factor passage (Passage 1 vs. Passage 2), the within-subjects factor recall test (first vs. second), and the between-subjects factor attention (full vs. divided) revealed a significant main effect of passage, 𝐵𝐵(1, 46) = 4.861, 𝑝𝑝 = .033, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .013 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 2.108, relative to the null model), and a significant main effect of recall test, 𝐵𝐵(1, 46) = 4.563, 𝑝𝑝 = .038, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .004 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 1.065, relative to the null model).

The main effect of attention was not significant, 𝐵𝐵(1,46) = 1.194, 𝑝𝑝 = .280, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .004 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 1.378, relative to the null model), and no significant interactions were observed, all 𝐵𝐵𝐹𝐹(1,46) ≤ 2.011, all 𝑝𝑝𝐹𝐹 ≥ .162. Overall, recall was higher in the first than in the second test, and Passage 2 was recalled better than Passage 1.

---Table 2 about here ---

Immediate Recall of Passage 3

For correct recall of Passage 3, a 2 × 2 ANOVA with the between-subjects factors interim activity (restudy vs. testing) and attention (full vs. divided) revealed a significant main effect of interim activity, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 14.128, 𝑝𝑝 < .001, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .120 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 30.252, relative to the null model), and a significant main effect of attention, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 16.400, 𝑝𝑝 < .001, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .138 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 72.154, relative to the null model). Importantly, the interaction between the two factors was also significant, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 6.239, 𝑝𝑝 = .014, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .052 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 3.590, relative to the two-main-effects model). Post-hoc comparisons indicated a reliable forward testing effect under full attention (5.71 vs. 3.02 recalled idea units), 𝑡𝑡(46) = 4.255, 𝑝𝑝 < .001, 𝑑𝑑 = 1.228 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵 10 > 100), but not under divided attention (2.90 vs. 2.35 recalled idea units), 𝑡𝑡(46) = 0.930, 𝑝𝑝 = .357, 𝑑𝑑 = 0.291 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 2.446; see Fig. 2).

---Figure 2 about here ---Regarding intrusions from prior passages in the Passage 3 recall test, a 2 × 2 ANOVA showed no significant main effect of interim activity, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 0.441, 𝑝𝑝 = .508, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝 2 < .001 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 3.844, relative to the null model), no significant main effect attention, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 2.403, 𝑝𝑝 = .125, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .014 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 1.611, relative to the null model), and also no significant interaction between the two factors, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 1.226, 𝑝𝑝 = .271, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .002 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 2.075, relative to the two-main-effects model). Thus, no reliable forward testing effect in intrusions was observed.

Final Knowledge Transfer Test

A 2 × 2 ANOVA with the between-subjects factors interim activity (restudy vs. testing) and attention (full vs. divided) revealed a significant main effect of interim activity, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 4.554, 𝑝𝑝 = .035, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .036 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 1.371, relative to the null model), and a significant main effect of attention, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 5.764, 𝑝𝑝 = .018, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .047 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 2.288, relative to the null model). The interaction between the two factors was only marginally significant, 𝐵𝐵(1, 92) = 3.290, 𝑝𝑝 = .073, 𝜔𝜔 𝑝𝑝2 = .023 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 0.882, relative to the two-main-effects model). Although the interaction did not reach significance, exploratory post-hoc comparisons were conducted to examine whether the pattern of results was consistent with the predicted forward testing effect under full attention. These comparisons showed that the forward testing effect in the final transfer test was significant under full attention (restudy: mean = 46.87%, SE = 4.96%; testing: mean = 62.29%, SE = 3.32%; 6.23 vs. 4.69 points), 𝑡𝑡(46) = 2.581, 𝑝𝑝 = .013, 𝑑𝑑 = 0.745 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵10 = 3.945), but not under divided attention (restudy: mean = 44.58%, SE = 2.95%; testing: mean = 45.83%, SE = 4.08%; 4.58 vs. 4.46 points), 𝑡𝑡(46) = 0.248, 𝑝𝑝 = .805, 𝑑𝑑 = 0.289 (𝐵𝐵𝐵𝐵01 = 3.393).

Discussion

Experiment 2 extended the investigation of the benefits of interim testing under divided attention to more educationally relevant learning materials by employing related prose passages instead of unrelated word lists. Replicating prior work with text materials, interim testing enhanced recall of the subsequently studied passage and improved performance on the final knowledge transfer test under full attention (e.g., Kriechbaum & Bäuml, in press; Szpunar et al., 2013; Wissman et al., 2011). Dividing attention during study of the target passage substantially impaired recall overall and eliminated the benefit of interim testing for immediate recall of Passage 3. A similar descriptive pattern emerged for the final knowledge transfer test, for which interim testing was beneficial under full attention but not under divided attention, although the interaction was only marginally significant. This pattern is unlikely to reflect a mere floor effect in the dividedattention condition, because recall remained clearly above zero and variances in Passage 3 recall did not differ significantly across conditions (Levene's test, 𝑝𝑝 = .594). No reliable effect of interim testing on prior-passage intrusions was observed. Together, these findings suggest that high attentional engagement during encoding of the target material is an important precondition for interim testing to benefit subsequent learning. Notably, divided attention had only a relatively small effect on recall performance in the restudy condition. This pattern may reflect differences between prose learning and list learning. In contrast to unrelated word lists, prose provides semantic structure and contextual support, which may render encoding more resilient to reductions in attentional resources. Importantly, however, although divided attention had only a limited effect on baseline prose learning, it eliminated the benefits of interim testing for subsequent learning. This pattern suggests that the mechanisms underlying the benefits of interim testing for subsequent learning may be more sensitive to reductions in attentional resources than baseline prose learning.

General Discussion

In two experiments, the present study examined whether the benefits of interim testing for subsequent learning depend on attentional engagement during encoding of the final target material. In both experiments, interim testing enhanced recall of the target material when attention during final encoding was left unaffected, thus replicating prior work with both word lists (e.g., Pastötter et al., 2011; Szpunar et al., 2008; Yang et al., 2019) and prose passages (e.g., Kriechbaum & Bäuml, 2024; Szpunar et al., 2013; Wissman et al., 2011). In contrast, when attention during encoding of the target material was experimentally reduced, the benefit of interim testing was attenuated in Experiment 1 and even eliminated in Experiment 2. Together, these results indicate that interim testing per se may not be sufficient to produce its full beneficial effect on subsequent learning. Rather, a high level of attentional engagement during encoding of the final material appears critical for interim testing to maximize learning of the material.

Several mechanisms have been proposed to explain how interim testing benefits subsequently studied material. For relatively simple materials such as word lists, the effect has been linked to changes in encoding or retrieval strategy (Chan et al., 2018; Cho et al., 2017), a reset of encoding (Pastötter et al., 2011 (Pastötter et al., , 2018)), and reduced proactive interference (Bäuml & Kliegl, 2013; Szpunar et al., 2008). For more complex prose materials, facilitated comprehension has been proposed as an additional mechanism, whereby prior testing promotes understanding and integration of subsequently studied information (Kriechbaum & Bäuml, in press; Szpunar et al., 2013; Wissman et al., 2011). Although all of these processes likely benefit from high attentional engagement during encoding of the final target material, they may do so to different degrees. The intrusion findings from Experiment 1 suggest that proactive-interference reduction is comparatively less affected by attentional distraction, because interim testing reduced prior-list intrusions regardless of attentional condition. By contrast, facilitated comprehension and integration may depend more strongly on attentional engagement. This interpretation is consistent with the stronger reduction of the benefits of interim testing under divided attention for related prose passages in Experiment 2 than for unrelated word lists in Experiment 1.

The present findings also extend those of Mulligan and Buchin (2025) by showing that, whereas divided attention during interim testing did not modulate the forward testing effect in their study, divided attention during encoding of the final target material did so in the present study.

Although the present study was not designed to directly compare attentional demands during encoding and retrieval, the observed pattern is broadly consistent with the divided-attention literature, which has repeatedly shown that attentional distraction during encoding typically impairs later memory more strongly than distraction during retrieval (Craik et al., 1996; Naveh-Benjamin et al., 2006; Yeung & Fernandes, 2021). In addition, the results of Experiment 2 further suggest that the benefits of interim testing may extend beyond immediate retention to performance on a final knowledge transfer test, replicating earlier work with related prose materials (Kriechbaum & Bäuml, in press; Szpunar et al, 2013). Under full attention, interim testing improved both recall of the target passage and transfer performance. Under divided attention, the numerical effect on transfer performance was markedly smaller and not statistically reliable. However, because the interaction in the transfer analysis did not reach conventional significance, the reduction of the transfer benefit under divided attention should be interpreted with caution. Nevertheless, the overall pattern is consistent with the possibility that attentional engagement during encoding constrains not only the retention-related but also the transferrelated benefits of interim testing.

From a practical perspective, the present findings suggest that interim testing can be a highly effective tool for promoting subsequent learning, but that its benefits depend on the attentional conditions under which new material is encoded. This issue is particularly relevant for application because, in everyday learning environments, learners may complete quizzes or selftests and then continue studying under less favorable attentional conditions. Importantly, attentional conditions during an ongoing learning episode are not necessarily stable but may change unexpectedly because of external or internal events. Such abrupt changes can substantially reduce the attentional resources available for continued learning and thereby impair learning and academic performance (May & Elder, 2018; van der Schuur et al., 2015). Although learners may not intentionally divide their attention only during the final phase of a study episode, the present manipulation should therefore be viewed as a controlled experimental analogue of such sudden changes in attentional conditions rather than as a literal simulation of a specific real-world learning scenario.

The present findings also help place the findings of Chan et al. (2025) into a broader context.

Chan et al. proposed that attentional engagement during online learning may already be comparatively low, such that stimuli that are distracting in a conventional sense, like entertaining onscreen videos, can under some conditions paradoxically re-engage learners instead of further reducing attention. Moreover, in their study, the elimination of the benefits of interim testing under distraction was driven by improved performance in the review condition rather than by impaired performance in the quiz condition. By contrast, in the present study, divided attention reduced the benefits of interim testing by impairing learning of the target material. Together, these findings suggest that the effects of attentional distraction depend on the timing, duration, and type of distraction, as well as the broader learning context. Future work should examine these factors more directly.

In conclusion, the present findings show that divided attention during encoding constrains the benefits of interim testing for subsequent learning. Interim testing enhanced learning of later studied material when attention during target encoding was left unaffected, but this benefit was reduced or even eliminated when attention was divided. These results identify a high level of attentional engagement during encoding of later studied information as an important precondition for interim testing to support later learning.

Data Accessibility Statement

Material, data, and analysis scripts are available on the Open Science Framework: https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/WDFHK.

Conflicts of Interest

Authors' Contributions

BP developed the study concept and experimental design, coordinated data collection, analyzed the data, and drafted the manuscript. KHB provided critical revisions. Both authors approved the final version of the manuscript for submission.

Each passage contained approximately 180 words and addressed fundamental mechanisms of human memory and forgetting (see https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/WDFHK). Specifically, the passages described (a) how forgetting can arise from competition between memory representations during retrieval, (b) how blocking mechanisms can resolve such retrieval competition, and (c) how inhibitory processes can suppress competing memories. The three passages were presented in a fixed order for all participants. Passages 1 and 2 served as practice materials during the interim manipulation phase, whereas Passage 3 served as the target material. To assess transfer of the learned material, a final knowledge test consisting of 10 open-ended questions identical to the test used in Experiment 2 ofKriechbaum and Bäuml (in press) was administered after the immediate recall test of Passage 3. The questions required short written responses and assessed both integration of information across the three passages and transfer of the learned concepts to new situations. The experiment was conducted using paper-and-pencil materials. Printed copies of the passages and answer sheets were provided to participants.For the divided-attention condition, a six-minute audio recording was used that consisted of a fictional news report about the results of the Summer Olympic Games. The report contained 60 single-digit numbers (30 even and 30 odd) referring to scores or statistics (see https://doi.org/10.17605/OSF.IO/WDFHK). The script was generated with the assistance of ChatGPT (OpenAI), and the audio was spoken and recorded by the first author. The recording was subsequently adjusted in playback speed to achieve a total duration of exactly 6 min and was played at medium volume via a smartphone placed next to the participant.
